It sounds real, like my husband is standing right next to me saying it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear anything else besides your name being called?\n\nPatient: No, not really anything else. It’s mainly my name being called. That’s what scared me, because it sounds so real, like my husband is right there, but when I check, nobody’s there.\n\nDoctor: Have you been using any alcohol or drugs around the time you hear your name being called?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I haven’t really connected it to anything like that. I wasn’t paying attention to alcohol or drugs when it happened.\n\nDoctor: Have you been drinking alcohol or using any drugs regularly?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to answer that. I mean, I’m not keeping track of anything like that, and I haven’t noticed a pattern. I wouldn’t say I’ve been thinking of it as a regular thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the voices you're hearing could be related to alcohol or drug use?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed it happening because of drinking or drugs. It just seems to happen on its own, as far as I can tell.\n\nDoctor: Do you hear your name being called all the time or just sometimes?\n\nPatient: Just sometimes. It’s not constant, but when it happens it feels very real. I’ll hear my name called from the hallway at home, and once it happened at the warehouse when nobody was there. That’s what scared me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever see things that aren't really there?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve been seeing things. It’s more hearing things—like my name being called from the hallway at home, and that time at the warehouse when nobody was there. It sounded real, like my husband was standing right next to me, and that’s what scared me.\n\nDoctor: Have you recently increased your use of alcohol or drugs?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve increased anything recently. I mean, nothing that I’ve noticed or that feels different than usual.\n\nDoctor: Has hearing your name being called affected your daily life or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s been getting in the way. At work I’ve made enough inventory mistakes that my supervisor pulled me aside about it. And at home, my husband has had to take over more of the bills and the regular routines because I’m not really keeping up. I’ve also stopped going to my church pantry shifts and book club, which really isn’t like me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: Yes. After all that running around and arguing, I just kind of dropped. I’ve been feeling really heavy and empty, like I’ll sit at the kitchen table and start crying over nothing. It feels like I can’t get myself back.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you felt extremely happy, energetic, or irritable and didn't need much sleep?\n\nPatient: Yes. There was a stretch where I was barely sleeping, maybe two or three hours a night, and I’d wake up feeling ready to get going. It felt like sleep was just getting in the way of all the things I wanted to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the voices you're hearing would stop if you stopped using alcohol or drugs?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t use drugs, and I only drink once in a while, so I haven’t noticed it changing based on that. The voices seem to happen either way.\n\nDoctor: How long did that stretch where you were barely sleeping and felt extremely happy and energetic last?\n\nPatient: It lasted about three weeks, I think. The whole thing, from being really wound up and restless to then hearing my name and crashing afterward, has been going on around that long.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

It’s not constant, but when it happens it feels very real. I’ll hear my name called from the hallway at home, and once it happened at the warehouse when nobody was there. That’s what scared me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever see things that aren't really there?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve been seeing things. It’s more hearing things—like my name being called from the hallway at home, and that time at the warehouse when nobody was there. The voices seem to happen either way.\n\nDoctor: How long did that stretch where you were barely sleeping and felt extremely happy and energetic last?\n\nPatient: It lasted about three weeks, I think. The whole thing, from being really wound up and restless to then hearing my name and crashing afterward, has been going on around that long.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel extremely happy or energetic during the three weeks you were barely sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yes. At first I felt really fired up, like I could do anything. I was putting all this energy into my craft business and getting really strict about things at home. It’s more hearing things—like my name being called from the hallway at home, and that time at the warehouse when nobody was there. It sounded real, like my husband was standing right next to me, and that’s what scared me.\n\nDoctor: Have you recently increased your use of alcohol or drugs?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve increased anything recently. I mean, nothing that I’ve noticed or that feels different than usual.\n\nDoctor: Has hearing your name being called affected your daily life or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s been getting in the way. At work I’ve made enough inventory mistakes that my supervisor pulled me aside about it. And at home, my husband has had to take over more of the bills and the regular routines because I’m not really keeping up. I’ve also stopped going to my church pantry shifts and book club, which really isn’t like me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: Yes. After all that running around and arguing, I just kind of dropped. I’ve been feeling really heavy and empty, like I’ll sit at the kitchen table and start crying over nothing. It feels like I can’t get myself back.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you felt extremely happy, energetic, or irritable and didn't need much sleep?\n\nPatient: Yes. There was a stretch where I was barely sleeping, maybe two or three hours a night, and I’d wake up feeling ready to get going. It felt like sleep was just getting in the way of all the things I wanted to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the voices you're hearing would stop if you stopped using alcohol or drugs?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t use drugs, and I only drink once in a while, so I haven’t noticed it changing based on that. The voices seem to happen either way.\n\nDoctor: How long did that stretch where you were barely sleeping and felt extremely happy and energetic last?\n\nPatient: It lasted about three weeks, I think. The whole thing, from being really wound up and restless to then hearing my name and crashing afterward, has been going on around that long.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel extremely happy or energetic during the three weeks you were barely sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yes. At first I felt really fired up, like I could do anything. I was putting all this energy into my craft business and getting really strict about things at home. But I was also irritable—if my husband made even a small comment, I’d snap at him like he was trying to stop me or hold me back.\n\nDoctor: Did your feelings of being extremely happy and energetic come before you started hearing your name being called?\n\nPatient: Yes, the feeling really energized and driven came first. The voice calling my name happened during that same stretch when I was barely sleeping and talking a lot. Before that, and after I got low and withdrawn, I wasn’t hearing voices like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your hearing your name being called is related to your use of alcohol?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed it happening because of drinking, or like right after drinking or anything. I just don’t know that it’s connected.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like you were on top of the world or invincible during those three weeks when you were barely sleeping and felt extremely happy and energetic?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I did. I really thought I had it all figured out. I was sure I could turn my crafts into a real retirement business right away, like it was going to work and everyone would see I had the perfect plan for our family.\n\nDoctor: Did your feelings of being extremely happy and energetic, and later feeling sad and empty, happen at the same time as hearing your name being called, or did they happen one after the other?\n\nPatient: They kind of happened one after the other. The voice calling my name was during that high-energy stretch, when I wasn’t sleeping much and I was talking nonstop and felt like I had to fix everything. Before that, and then later when I got really low and withdrawn, I wasn’t hearing voices or having thoughts like that.\n\nDoctor: Did the voice calling your name stop when you transitioned from feeling extremely happy and energetic to feeling sad and empty?\n\nPatient: Yes, it stopped when that high-energy time ended. It was happening when I was barely sleeping and talking nonstop, feeling like I had to fix everything. Once I got into that low, withdrawn period, I wasn’t hearing my name or having things like that anymore.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

It feels like I can’t get myself back.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you felt extremely happy, energetic, or irritable and didn't need much sleep?\n\nPatient: Yes. There was a stretch where I was barely sleeping, maybe two or three hours a night, and I’d wake up feeling ready to get going. It felt like sleep was just getting in the way of all the things I wanted to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the voices you're hearing would stop if you stopped using alcohol or drugs?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t use drugs, and I only drink once in a while, so I haven’t noticed it changing based on that. The voices seem to happen either way.\n\nDoctor: How long did that stretch where you were barely sleeping and felt extremely happy and energetic last?\n\nPatient: It lasted about three weeks, I think. The whole thing, from being really wound up and restless to then hearing my name and crashing afterward, has been going on around that long.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel extremely happy or energetic during the three weeks you were barely sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yes. At first I felt really fired up, like I could do anything. I was putting all this energy into my craft business and getting really strict about things at home. But I was also irritable—if my husband made even a small comment, I’d snap at him like he was trying to stop me or hold me back.\n\nDoctor: Did your feelings of being extremely happy and energetic come before you started hearing your name being called?\n\nPatient: Yes, the feeling really energized and driven came first. The voice calling my name happened during that same stretch when I was barely sleeping and talking a lot. Before that, and after I got low and withdrawn, I wasn’t hearing voices like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your hearing your name being called is related to your use of alcohol?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed it happening because of drinking, or like right after drinking or anything. I just don’t know that it’s connected.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like you were on top of the world or invincible during those three weeks when you were barely sleeping and felt extremely happy and energetic?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I did. I really thought I had it all figured out. I was sure I could turn my crafts into a real retirement business right away, like it was going to work and everyone would see I had the perfect plan for our family.\n\nDoctor: Did your feelings of being extremely happy and energetic, and later feeling sad and empty, happen at the same time as hearing your name being called, or did they happen one after the other?\n\nPatient: They kind of happened one after the other. The voice calling my name was during that high-energy stretch, when I wasn’t sleeping much and I was talking nonstop and felt like I had to fix everything. Before that, and then later when I got really low and withdrawn, I wasn’t hearing voices or having thoughts like that.\n\nDoctor: Did the voice calling your name stop when you transitioned from feeling extremely happy and energetic to feeling sad and empty?\n\nPatient: Yes, it stopped when that high-energy time ended. It was happening when I was barely sleeping and talking nonstop, feeling like I had to fix everything. Once I got into that low, withdrawn period, I wasn’t hearing my name or having things like that anymore.\n\nDoctor: Did your period of feeling extremely happy and energetic, with barely any sleep, last for at least one week?\n\nPatient: Yes, it was around three weeks altogether. The whole thing—from being wound up and restless, to hearing my name, and then crashing after—went on about that long."
